Reasoning as in the case of a mixture of Gaussians allows to establish the variant of
Equation

62
with
Z^{\prime}_{p}
required in
Theorem

26
.
However, in this setting,
\psi(\omega)f_{0}(\omega)
is no longer sub-exponential hence establishing
Equation

64
in
Theorem

26
requires more work and a choice of
M>0
that depends at most polynomially on the sketch size
m
. As detailed in
Section

A.9.4
, we get the following result as a corollary of

Theorem

26
.

Corollary 28
.

Consider
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, an integer
k\geq 1
, a scale
s>0
, and

\epsilon\geq s(4\sqrt{\log(5ek)}).

(75)

With
\mathcal{T}
,
\kappa
,
\bm{\Sigma}
as in
Example

7
and
\mathcal{A}
the
w
-RFF sketching operator with “flat”
\kappa
-compatible weight function
w\equiv 1
and
m
i.i.d. frequencies
\omega_{j}\sim\Lambda:=\mathcal{N}(0,s^{-2}\mathbb{I}_{d})
, where
\mathbb{I}_{d}
is the identity matrix of dimension
d
, the mutual coherence of
\kappa
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
is bounded by
\mu
where
0<\mu<\frac{1}{10k}
.

Moreover, for each
0<\tau<1-5c
, where
c:=(2k-1)\mu
, we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\delta(\mathcal{S}_{k}|\mathcal{A})>\frac{4c+\tau}{1-c}\bigg)\leq 11\exp\Big(-\frac{m}{v}\Big)\big(1+C(\tau,m)/\tau)^{3d+2},

(76)

where

\displaystyle v=v_{k}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=512k^{2}\left((C_{0}/\tau)^{2}+\frac{1}{3}(C_{0}/\tau)\right),\ \text{with}\ C_{0}\leq 7s^{-2}\max(1,\sqrt{3}\epsilon^{2}),

(77)

\displaystyle C(\tau,m)

\displaystyle:=\left(6144(1+2s^{-1})^{3}\max(1,\sqrt{3}\epsilon^{2})(2d^{3/2}+\sqrt{m}\tau^{3/2})\right)\cdot k(1+\operatorname{diam}_{a}(\Theta)).

(78)

As in the case of Gaussian mixtures, and in contrast to
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
, Theorem 6.11)
, the RIP constant is not guaranteed to be (with high probability) arbitrarily close to zero: it can only be made arbitrarily close to
4c/(1-c)<1
. This is the price we pay for being able to handle plain importance sampling with
w\equiv 1
.
Observe that
1+C(\tau,m)/\tau\leq\sqrt{m}(1+C(\tau,1)/\tau)\leq m(1+C(\tau,1)/\tau)
hence the r.h.s. of (
76
) is upper bounded by

11\exp\bigg(-\frac{m}{v}\Big(1-(3d+2)v\frac{\log(m)}{m}\Big)+(3d+2)\log\big(1+C(\tau,1)/\tau\big)\Big)\bigg).

We deduce that for
\eta\in(0,1]
, and for the
w-RFF
sketching operator described in Corollary
28
, the probability that the event
\big\{\delta(\mathcal{S}_{k}|\mathcal{A})\leq(4c+\tau)/(1-c)\big\}
holds is larger than
1-\eta
, as soon as

\frac{m}{\log m}\gtrsim(3d+2)v\log\big(1+C(\tau,1)/\tau\big)+\log(11/\eta)=\Omega(k^{2}d).

In other words, a sufficient sketch size is
\mathcal{O}(k^{2}d)
: our analysis allows to obtain the same dependencies on
k
and
d

as the analysis developed in
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
but
without
assuming
the conditions (
28
) that imposes constraints on the importance sampling weight
w
.
It would be tempting to think that a judicious choice of the weight function
w
would allow to further improve the dependency on
k
of the sketch size from
\mathcal{O}(k^{2}d)
to
\mathcal{O}(kd)
. Unfortunately, as we shall see in Section
3.4
, our analysis does not allow us to make such an improvement. The investigation of the role of the weight function is deferred for future work.
Finally, observe that the constant
\log(1+C(\tau,1)/\tau)
depends logarithmically on
D
(the diameter of
\Theta
), on
s^{-1}
, and on
d\log k
.
Note that the logarithmic dependency on
D
is well known in the Fourier features literature
(
Sriperumbudur and Szabó 2015
)
, while the dependency on
s^{-1}
has empirical implications: the parameter
s
should be choosen small enough so that the mutual coherence of
\kappa
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
is bounded by
\mu
, yet not too small. Interestingly, this phenomenon was documented in several empirical investigations
(
Keriven et al. 2017
;
Chatalic 2020
)
.

3.3.1
Towards bounds for structured random sketching

A benefit of the theoretical analysis presented in this work is to pave the way to
theoretical guarantees on the RIP of sketching operators based on structured features.
Indeed, as evoked in
Section

2.5
, there are now many constructions of structured random Fourier features
where
m
is a multiple of
d
and the frequencies
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{m}
are block-i.i.d.
with blocks of size
b=d
: the matrix
\bm{\Omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
with columns
\omega_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq m
is the concatenation of i.i.d. random matrices
\bm{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
1\leq i\leq m/d
.

Such constructions can be designed to lead to (non independent but) identically distributed Gaussian frequencies
\omega_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{d})
, so that the average marginal density satisfies (
22
) with a Gaussian kernel and the simplest
\kappa
-compatible weight function
w\equiv 1
, see e.g.
(
Chatalic 2020
, Chapter 5)
. This allows to reuse the proof techniques used to establish
Corollary

27
-
Corollary

28
, showing as an intermediate step that each
X_{j}:=\psi(\omega_{j})(\sqrt{C_{\kappa}}|\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle|)^{p}

(resp.
X_{j}:=(\sqrt{C_{\kappa}}|\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle|)^{p}
, when
B_{\psi}<+\infty
), with arbitrary
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisifying
\|x\|_{a}=1
and
p\in\{0,1,2\}
, is sub-exp(
\nu,\beta
) with
|\mathbb{E}X|\leq B
.

Corollary 29
.

Let
m
be a multiple of
d
.

•

Consider the same setting as in
Corollary

27
, and assume that the frequencies
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{m}
are
block-i.i.d.

with blocks of size
b=d
such that the
\omega_{j}
are identically (but not necessarily independently) distributed Gaussian frequencies
\omega_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}/s^{2})
. Then, for each
0<\tau<1-5c
, where
c:=(2k-1)\mu
, we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\delta(\mathcal{S}_{k}|\mathcal{A})>\frac{4c+\tau}{1-c}\bigg)\leq 11\exp\Big(-\frac{m}{dv}\Big)\big(1+C/\tau)^{3d+2},

(79)

where
v,C
are defined as in
Corollary

27
.

•

Consider the same setting as in
Corollary

28
, and assume that the frequencies
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{m}
are block-i.i.d. with blocks of size
b=d
such that the
\omega_{j}
are identically (but not necessarily independently) distributed Gaussian frequencies
\omega_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s^{-2}\mathbb{I}_{d})
. Then, for each
0<\tau<1-5c
, where
c:=(2k-1)\mu
, we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\delta(\mathcal{S}_{k}|\mathcal{A})>\frac{4c+\tau}{1-c}\bigg)\leq(10+d)\exp\Big(-\frac{m}{dv}\Big)\big(1+C(\tau,m/d)/\tau)^{3d+2},

(80)

where
v,C
are defined as in
Corollary

28
.

The proof of
Corollary

29
is given in
Section

A.9.5
. While this result does not assume the independence of the random variables
X_{j}
, it comes with a non-negligible cost: compared to (
71
) and (
76
), the ”variance” term (
64
) in (
79
) and (
80
) worsens by a factor
d
compared to the fully i.i.d. setting. There is also a more benign
\log d
factor in sketch sizes due to the appearance of the
(10+d)
factor in (
80
). Thus, a refined analysis of the expectation and of the sub-exponentiality constants of the random variables
Z_{p}
and
Z^{\prime}_{p}
(cf (
62
)-(
68
)) is required in order to prove competitive bounds on sketch sizes for structured sketching.
Ideally, we may hope to prove that
Z_{p}
and
Z^{\prime}_{p}
are sub-exp(
\nu,\beta
) with
\nu=\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{d})
and
\beta=\mathcal{O}(1/d)
for some families of structured random matrices.
Note however that this would require to slightly sharpen the bound obtained in
Theorem

26
. Indeed, the main bottleneck in the sketch size would be the constant
\nu^{\prime}
defined in (
64
) that involves the term
B
which is a constant independent of
d
. Improving
Theorem

26
would either require to refine
Lemma

25
(using more taylored assumptions) in order to circumvent the presence of this constant in
\nu^{\prime}
, or to more directly rely on
Theorem

22
and in establishing autonomous concentration bounds. This is left to future work.

3.4
Lower bounds

To conclude, we provide several lower bounds that complete the picture established in this section.

First, we show that condition (
28
), which is known to be sufficient to control the covering numbers appearing in
Theorem

14
, is indeed close to necessary for these covering numbers to be well-defined and finite. This shows that existing theory (such as
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
, Theorem 5.13)
) is simply too restrictive to provide guarantees for perhaps the most natural setting where there is “no” importance sampling, i.e.,
w\equiv 1
, which is in contrast covered by our new results.

Second, we investigate the gap between sufficient sketch sizes endowed with theoretical guarantees, which scale as
O(k^{2}d)
, and practically observed sketch sizes, which scale as
O(kd)
. We demonstrate that a proof route which could seem natural to bridge this gap is in fact a dead-end, leaving possible improvements to further work.

3.4.1
Lower bounds on variance terms

The empirical investigations in
(
Keriven et al. 2017
)
showed that a practically sufficient sketch size scales as
\mathcal{O}(dk)
compared to the theoretically sufficient sketch size
\mathcal{O}(dk^{2})
obtained by the analysis given in
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
and the analysis given in this work. This suggest that there is still room for improvement on the theoretical bounds of sketching. We investigate below theoretical approaches that may seem natural ways to improve the proof techniques respectively introduced in
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
and in this work. Our main conclusion is that these approaches
cannot
lead to the desired explanation of the empirical findings of
(
Keriven et al. 2017
)
.

Limits of the proof technique of
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)

After a careful examination of the proof given in
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
,
it may be tempting to improve the concentration inequality (
27
) and target one of the form

\forall\tau>0,\ \>\>\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\|\mathcal{A}\nu\|_{2}^{2}-1\big|>\frac{\tau}{2}\Big)\leq 2\exp\bigg(-\frac{m}{v_{0}(\tau)}\bigg),

with
v_{0}(\tau)

independent of
k
(under appropriate incoherence assumptions on
\kappa
, that depend of
k
). This would indeed easily provide the desired result by combining the technical ingredients as in the proof of
Theorem

14
, however under standard assumptions
10
10

10

A subgaussian tail or a sub-exponential tail.
on the growth of
v_{0}(\tau)
when
\tau\to\infty
, it is a classical exercice
11
11

11

See
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 2.3)
and
(
Bousquet et al. 2020
, Lemma 1)
.
to show that this implies bounded moments
\mathbb{E}\|\mathcal{A}\nu\|_{2}^{2q}
, for
q\geq 2
, depending only on
v_{0}(\tau)
and
m
, in particular this would also imply the existence of a constant
C>0
,
independent of

k
, such that

\forall\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k},\>\>\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\nu\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{C}{m}.

where
\mathbb{V}(\cdot)
denotes the variance of a scalar random variable.
However, as we now show, under typical assumptions on the
2k
-coherence of the kernel, this variance grows linearly with
k
.

We begin with a technical lemma proved in
Section

A.1
.

Lemma 30
.

Consider a normalized shift-invariant kernel
\kappa
and
\pi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
w:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,+\infty)
is
\kappa
-compatible and satisfies

\int\big|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{1},\pi_{0}\rangle\big|^{4}w^{-2}(\omega)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega<+\infty,

(81)

then the following shift-invariant kernel is well-defined

\displaystyle\kappa_{w}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{1},\pi_{0}\rangle\big|^{4}w^{-2}(\omega)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)e^{\imath\omega^{\top}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}\omega,\quad\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(82)

and satisfies
\kappa_{w}(0,0)\geq\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}
. The following weight function
is
\kappa
-compatible and satisfies (
81
)

w_{0}(\omega):=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{-1}\cdot|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{1},\pi_{0}\rangle|.

(83)

Moreover
\kappa_{w_{0}}(0,0)=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}
and more generally

\kappa_{w_{0}}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}\langle\pi_{\theta},\pi_{\theta^{\prime}}\rangle_{\kappa},\quad\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

In the special case of a Dirac base distribution
\pi_{0}
, (
83
) simply defines a “flat” weight function
w_{0}\equiv 1
since
\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}=\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{0}\rangle_{\kappa}=\kappa(0,0)=1=|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{1},\pi_{0}\rangle|
.

Theorem 31
.

Consider a normalized shift-invariant kernel
\kappa
.
Consider a location-based family
\mathcal{T}
with base distribution
\pi_{0}
, an integer
k\geq 1
, and the
separated
k
-mixture model
\mathfrak{G}_{k}
from (
8
) where
\varrho(\theta,\theta^{\prime}):=\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|
for some arbitrary norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Consider a vector
\theta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|\theta^{*}\|\geq 1
, and observe that the following two
k
-mixtures are
1
-separated with respect to
\varrho
, i.e.,
\nu_{k,1},\nu_{k,2}\in\mathfrak{G}_{k}
, so that
\nu_{k}:=(\nu_{k,1}-\nu_{k,2})/\|\nu_{k,1}-\nu_{k,2}\|_{\kappa}\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
:

\nu_{k,1}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{k}\pi_{(2i-2)\theta^{*}},\>\>\nu_{k,2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{k}\pi_{(2i-1)\theta^{*}}.

1.

If the
2k
-coherence of
\kappa
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
is bounded by
0\leq c<1
then for any
\kappa
-compatible weight function
w
that satisfies (
81
),
the
w
-RFF sketching operator
\mathcal{A}
with
m
i.i.d frequencies
\omega_{j}\sim\Lambda:=w^{2}\hat{\kappa}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{m}\Big(C_{w}k-1\Big),

(84)

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad C_{w}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\kappa_{w}(0,0)}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}}\cdot\frac{4/3-2c_{w}}{(1+c)^{2}}\,\qquad\text{with}\ c_{w}:=2k\sup_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}:\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\geq 1}\frac{|\kappa_{w}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})|}{\kappa_{w}(0,0)}.

(85)

2.

If the mutual coherence of
\kappa
is bounded by
c/2k
we have
c_{w_{0}}\leq c
with
w_{0}
as in (
83
).

The proof of
Theorem

31
is given in
Section

A.2
. For a kernel with mutual coherence bounded by
c/2k
with
c\leq 1/2
, we obtain
\frac{4/3-2c_{w_{0}}}{(1+c)^{2}}\geq(4/3-1)/(3/2)^{2}=4/27\geq 1/7
. Since
\kappa_{w_{0}}(0,0)/\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}=1
(by Lemma
30
) we get
C_{w_{0}}\geq 1/7
.
Finally, since the mutual coherence of
\kappa
is bounded by
c/2k
, its
2k
-coherence is bounded by
c
(cf (
15
)), and (
84
) implies
that the
w_{0}
-RFF sketching operator
\mathcal{A}
with
m
i.i.d frequencies
\omega_{j}\sim\Lambda_{0}:=w_{0}^{2}\hat{\kappa}
satisfies

\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|^{2}\geq\frac{k/7-1}{m}.

(86)

In the special case of a mixture of Diracs, since
w_{0}\equiv 1
, it is not difficult to check that
\kappa_{w_{0}}=\kappa
. For a non-negative kernel
\kappa\geq 0
, the fact that
\kappa_{w_{0}}\geq 0
allows to improve an intermediate bound (in Equation (
93
) in the proof), leading to the same result where

4/3-2c_{w_{0}}
is replaced with
4/3-c_{w_{0}}
in the definition of
C_{w_{0}}
. This shows that (
86
) is then valid even with a mutual coherence bounded by
c/2k
with
c<1
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

31
, with
w=w_{0}
, we have
\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|^{2}=\Omega(k/m)
. This implies that with this weight function, and even with the usual incoherence assumption, the term
v(k,\tau)
in (
27
)
cannot
be bounded from above by a universal constant that is independent of
k
.
This result highlights the difference between classical compressed sensing and sketching. Indeed, if we consider a random Gaussian matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
with i.i.d. entries
\mathcal{N}(0,1/m)
, it is well known that for every normalized vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
(such that
\|x\|_{2}=1
) the variance of
\|Ax\|_{2}^{2}

does not depend on the sparsity

k
of the vector
x
;
(see e.g.
Foucart and Rauhut 2013
, Lemma 9.8)
.

Figure

2
illustrates this claim: we compare the variance of
\|Ax_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
where
x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a normalized vector of sparsity
k

to the variance of
\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
where
\mathcal{A}
and
\nu_{k}
are defined in
Theorem

31
, with
\pi_{0}
the Dirac distribution,
w=w_{0}\equiv 1
, and the
2k
-coherence of
\kappa
is smaller than
1/2
.
We observe that the variance of
\|Ax_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
is practically flat as a function of
k
, while the variance of
\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
is linear in
k
. This observation shows that the study of the RIP in the set of mixtures of Diracs
\mathfrak{G}_{k}
is not a mere extension of the existing RIP literature in Euclidean spaces.

Figure 2
:
The term
m\times\mathbb{V}\|Ax_{k}\|^{2}
(left) compared to the term
m\times\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|^{2}
(right).

Limits of the proof technique proposed in this paper.

Now, a careful examination of the analysis leading to
Corollary

27
and
Corollary

28
suggests that the unwanted
O(k^{2}d)
instead of
O(kd)
behaviour of the sufficient sketch size results from the requirement of concentration inequality (
59
) in Theorem
22
.
In particular, in order to improve the theoretical guarantees of sketching using i.i.d. random frequencies, it would be tempting to seek a weight function
w
such that the random variable

\psi_{\ell}(z|\omega):=\psi(\omega)f_{\ell}(z|\omega)

is sub-exp(
\nu_{\ell},b_{\ell}
) with
\nu_{\ell}=\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{k})
, for each
\ell\in\{\mathrm{mm,md,dd}\}
and
z\in\Theta_{\ell}
.

The reader can check that such an approach would indeed allow to establish guarantees with a sketch size
\mathcal{O}(kd)
.
However, this would also imply that for
\ell\in\{\mathrm{mm,md,dd}\}
and
z\in\Theta_{\ell}
, the variance of
\psi_{\ell}(z|\omega)
would satisfy
\mathbb{V}\psi_{\ell}(z|\omega)=\mathcal{O}(1/k)
.
Now, when
\kappa
has mutual coherence bounded by
\mu<1/(2k-1)
(this is a natural assumption in our context), the expectation of this variable satisfies
\mathbb{E}\psi_{\ell}(z|\omega)=\mathcal{O}(1/k)
, hence we would obtain that

\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\ell}^{2}(z|\omega)]=\mathcal{O}(1/k).

(87)

The following result shows that (
87
)
cannot hold
in the specific setting of mixture of Diracs.

Proposition 32
.

Consider
\mathcal{T}
to be a location-based family with the dirac in
0
as a base distribution, and consider
\kappa
to be a normalized shift-invariant kernel such that
\kappa\geq 0
. Consider
\phi_{\omega}
as defined in (
17
), then for any
\kappa
-compatible weight function
w
and for
\omega\sim\Lambda:=w^{2}\hat{\kappa}
, we have

\forall y\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}},\>\>\>\>\mathbb{E}[\psi^{2}_{\mathrm{mm}}(y|\omega)]\geq\frac{1}{4}.

This lower bound holds
irrespective of how small the mutual coherence of
\kappa
may be.

Proof.

Let
y\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
. Since
\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}=\kappa(0,0)=1
and
|\langle\pi_{0},\phi_{\omega}\rangle|=1/w(\omega)
, by (
36
)-(
38
) we have

\psi_{\mathrm{mm}}(y|\omega):=\psi(\omega)f_{\mathrm{mm}}(y|\omega)=\frac{|\langle\pi_{0},\phi_{\omega}\rangle|^{2}}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}\cos(\langle\omega,y\rangle)=\frac{\cos(\langle\omega,y\rangle)}{w^{2}(\omega)}.

As
w
is
\kappa
-compatible, we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w^{2}(\omega)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega=1
, thus by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Lambda}[\psi^{2}_{\mathrm{mm}}(y|\omega)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\cos^{2}(\langle\omega,y\rangle)}{w^{4}(\omega)}w^{2}(\omega)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w^{2}(\omega)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\cos^{2}(\langle\omega,y\rangle)}{w^{2}(\omega)}\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle\geq\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\cos(\langle\omega,y\rangle)|\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\cos^{2}(\langle\omega,y\rangle)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\Big)^{2}.

Finally, observe that
\cos(\langle\omega,y\rangle)^{2}=(1+\cos(2\langle\omega,y\rangle))/2
, so that we have, using that
\kappa\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Lambda}[\psi^{2}_{\mathrm{mm}}(y|\omega)]\geq\Big[\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\cos(2\langle\omega,y\rangle)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega\bigg)\Big]^{2}=\Big(\frac{1}{2}(1+\kappa(2y,0))\Big)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}.

∎

Figure 3
:
An illustration of the lower bound of Proposition
32
(left) for three choices of
w
(right):
w_{0}(\omega)=1,\>\>w_{1}(\omega)=(1+\|\omega\|)^{-1},\>\>w_{2}(\omega)=(\|\omega\|^{4}+1)(\|\omega\|^{6}+1)^{-1}
.

Proposition
32
shows that improving the dependency of the sketch size on
m
cannot simply rely on improved concentration bounds: obtaining sharper bounds on sketch sizes that reflect the empirical findings of
(
Keriven et al. 2017
)
seems to require a substantially subtler analysis which is beyond the scope of this paper.

4
Conclusion

In this work we revisited the theoretical analysis of the Restricted Isometry Property for sketching operators proposed in
(
Gribonval et al. 2021b
;
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
. This property is crucial in the field of compressive learning: it measures how the sketching operator preserves the MMD distance between measures belonging to a model set of measures. In particular, the sketching operators proposed in
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
are suited for models of mixtures and are based on Fourier features. Nevertheless, the proposed theoretical analysis makes some additional assumptions that are summarized by the conditions (
28
).
After investigating the partial necessity of the conditions (
28
) in the analysis of
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
, we proposed an alternative analysis based on deterministic bounds of
\delta(\mathcal{S}_{k}|\mathcal{A})
, then we showed how to leverage these deterministic bounds to establish the Restricted Isometry Property for stochastic sketching operators restricted to sets of mixtures based on location based measures. In particular, we showed that our revisited analysis allows to deal with realistic settings not covered by
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
.

Beyond these contributions, this work opens the door to further developments on the theoretical study of sketching operators used in the context of compressive learning. For instance, in the context of structured sketching introduced in
(
Chatalic et al. 2018
)
, the frequencies are rather block-i.i.d. samples but not i.i.d. samples (cf the end of Section
2.5
). Theorem
22
remains valid in this context: indeed this result can be used without assuming that the frequencies
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{m}
are i.i.d.. The main hindrance remaining on this direction is to check that the punctual concentration expressed by conditions (
57
), (
58
), (
59
) hold even when using block-i.i.d. frequencies. For this purpose, the existing results on the literature may help
(
Le et al. 2013
;
Choromanski and Sindhwani 2016
;
Choromanski et al. 2017
;
Munkhoeva et al. 2018
)
.
Another setting where our results may be useful is the study of deterministic sketching operators. Indeed, as shown in Section
3
, the core of our analysis is based on deterministic bounds of
\delta(\mathcal{S}_{k}|\mathcal{A})
presented in Section
3.2
, and recent years have witnessed an increased interest into the theoretical study of
deterministic Fourier feature maps

(
Dao et al. 2017
;
Yang et al. 2014
)
. Investigating whether deterministic sketching operators still satisfy the same guarantees as the stochastic ones is thus both a natural and challenging question.

As shown in Section
3.4
, neither the analysis of
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
nor our analysis unfortunately achieves to explain the empirical findings of
(
Keriven et al. 2017
)
, and there remains a gap between sufficient sketch sizes endowed with theoretical guarantees, which scale as
\mathcal{O}(k^{2}d)
, and practically observed sketch sizes, which scale as
\mathcal{O}(kd)
. On the one hand, the quadratic theoretical dependency on the ’sparsity’
k
is not surprising given the known limits of sparse recovery guarantees exploiting dictionary coherence
(
Foucart and Rauhut 2013
, Chapter 5)
. Yet, the literature on compressive sensing manages to establish bounds essentially linear in the sparsity using random matrix techniques that do rely on mutual coherence
(
Foucart and Rauhut 2013
, Chapter 9)
. The proofs in this field exploit a fine study of the eigenvalues of random matrices, which was until now somehow overlooked in the community of compressive learning. Thus, an interesting direction of research is the study of the eigenvalues of the random matrices that appear in this context. Recent developments on the study of ridge kernel regression for random Fourier features may help
(
Avron et al. 2017
;
Li et al. 2019
)
.
In particular, in this line of research, the authors investigated the impact of the frequency distribution in the quality of the approximations based on random Fourier features. The techniques developed in these works may be helpful to understand the impact of the frequency distribution in the design of sketching operators. In the same vein, alternative frequency distributions, that define other kernels than the Gaussian kernel, have manifested better empirical performance when used in sketching-based learning tasks such as mixture learning; see Section 4.2 in
(
Chatalic 2020
)
for an example. This motivates to scrutinize the impact of the kernel on the design of the sketching operator.

This project was supported by the AllegroAssai ANR project ANR-19-CHIA-0009. The authors
would like to thank Titouan Vayer for his constructive feedback on an early version of this work and for sketching an early version of
Lemma

38
. Rémi Gribonval would like to thank Felix Krahmer for interesting discussions at the Oberwolfach Workshop 2148.

A.2
Proof of
Theorem

31

The proof relies on the following result which gives a closed formula of the variance of interest.

Proposition 33
.

Consider a normalized shift-invariant kernel
\kappa
, a
\kappa
-compatible positive weight function
w
satisfying (
81
),
\Lambda=w^{2}\hat{\kappa}
and
\mathcal{A}
a
\kappa
-compatible random
w
-FF sketching operator as in
Example

12
with
m
i.i.d. frequencies. Consider a location-based family
\mathcal{T}
with base distribution
\pi_{0}
.
Given any location parameters
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{2k}\in\Theta
and weights
u_{1},\dots,u_{2k}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\sum_{i\in[2k]}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\Big((\bm{u}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}-\big\|\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\big\|_{\kappa}^{4}\Big),

(89)

where
\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}\in\mathbb{R}^{(2k)^{2}}
is the tensor product with itself of the vector
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{2k}
containing the
u_{i}
, and

\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)=[\kappa_{w}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}},\theta_{i_{4}}-\theta_{i_{3}})]_{(i_{1},i_{2}),(i_{3},i_{4})\in[2k]^{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(2k)^{2}\times(2k)^{2}}

(90)

with
[2k]:=\{1,2,\dots,2k\}
and
\kappa_{w}
is the shift-invariant kernel defined in (
82
).

Proof of
Proposition

33
.

As
\|\mathcal{A}\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j\in[m]}\big|\langle\phi_{\omega_{j}}^{w},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle\big|^{2}

is the average of
m
i.i.d. variables, we have

\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{V}\big|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{w},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle\big|^{2}

so we now characterize

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{w},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Lambda}\big|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{w},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle\big|^{4}-\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Lambda}\big|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{w},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle\big|^{2}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Lambda}\big|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{w},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle\big|^{4}-\big\|\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\big\|_{\kappa}^{4}

where we used (
21
) since
w
is
\kappa
-compatible and
\Lambda=w^{2}\hat{\kappa}
(see Example
12
). Given the expression (
17
) of
\phi_{\omega}:=\phi_{\omega}^{w}=\phi^{1}_{\omega}/w(\omega)
and since
\{\pi_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
is location-based,

\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{\theta}\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\pi_{\theta}}\phi_{\omega}(X)=\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime}\sim\pi_{0}}\phi_{\omega}(X^{\prime}+\theta)=e^{\imath\omega^{\top}\theta}\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime}\sim\pi_{0}}\phi_{\omega}(X^{\prime})=e^{\imath\omega^{\top}\theta}\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle,

so that we can develop

\displaystyle\big|\langle\phi_{\omega},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle\big|^{4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{4}=1}^{2k}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{\theta_{i_{1}}}\rangle\overline{\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{\theta_{i_{2}}}\rangle}\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{\theta_{i_{3}}}\rangle\overline{\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{\theta_{i_{4}}}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{4}=1}^{2k}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}|\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle|^{4}e^{\imath\big(\omega^{\top}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}}+\theta_{i_{3}}-\theta_{i_{4}})\big)}.

Moreover, given the expression (
19
) of the pdf
\Lambda(\omega)
we have
for every
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[2k]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim\Lambda}|\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle|^{4}e^{\imath\big(\omega(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}}+\theta_{i_{3}}-\theta_{i_{4}})\big)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\langle\phi_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle|^{4}e^{\imath\big(\omega^{\top}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}}+\theta_{i_{3}}-\theta_{i_{4}})\big)}w^{2}(\omega)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)d\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\langle\phi_{\omega}^{1},\pi_{0}\rangle|^{4}w^{-4}(\omega)e^{\imath\big(\omega^{\top}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}}+\theta_{i_{3}}-\theta_{i_{4}})\big)}w^{2}(\omega)\hat{\kappa}(\omega)d\omega

\displaystyle=\kappa_{w}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}},\theta_{i_{4}}-\theta_{i_{3}}),

where
\kappa_{w}
is defined in (
82
). As a result, we have

\mathbb{E}\big|\langle\phi_{\omega},\sum_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\rangle\big|^{4}=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{2k}\sum_{i_{4}=1}^{2k}u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}u_{i_{3}}u_{i_{4}}\kappa_{w}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}},\theta_{i_{4}}-\theta_{i_{3}})=(\bm{u}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}

where, according to the notations of the proposition
\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}\in\mathbb{R}^{(2k)^{2}}
is the vector with entries

u^{\otimes 2}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}=u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}
for each pair
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[2k]\times[2k]
, and
\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)
is the square matrix of size
(2k)^{2}\times(2k)^{2}
defined in (
90
).
Putting the pieces together yields (
89
) as claimed.
∎

By Proposition
33

with
\nu_{k}:=\mu_{k,1}-\mu_{k,2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2k}u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}
, where
u_{i}:=\tfrac{(-1)^{i-1}}{k}
,
\theta_{i}:=(i-1)\theta^{*}
,

\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|^{2}=\frac{\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}(\mu_{k,1}-\mu_{k,2})\|^{2}}{\|\mu_{k,1}-\mu_{k,2}\|_{\kappa}^{4}}=\frac{1}{m}\Bigg(\frac{(\bm{u}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}}{\|\mu_{k,1}-\mu_{k,2}\|_{\kappa}^{4}}-1\Bigg).

(91)

We now bound
\|\mu_{k,1}-\mu_{k,2}\|_{\kappa}^{4}
and
(\bm{u}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}
to get the first part of the Theorem.

Bounding
\|\mu_{k,1}-\mu_{k,2}\|_{\kappa}^{4}
.

Since
\kappa
is shift-invariant and
\mathcal{T}
is a location-based family we have
\|\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|_{\kappa}^{2}=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}
for each
i\in[2k]
. Since
\varrho(\theta_{i},\theta_{j})=\|\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}\|=|i-j|\cdot\|\theta^{*}\|\geq 1
for
1\leq i\neq j\leq 2k
, the
2k
(unnormalized) monopoles
\{u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{2k}
are pairwise
1
-separated dipoles with respect to
\rho
. As
\kappa
has its
2k
-coherence with respect to
\mathcal{T}
bounded by
c
(cf Definition
4
-(
12
)), it follows that

\frac{2}{k}(1-c)\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}=(1-c)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2k}\|u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|_{\kappa}^{2}\leq\|\mu_{1,k}-\mu_{2,k}\|_{\kappa}^{2}\leq(1+c)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2k}\|u_{i}\pi_{\theta_{i}}\|_{\kappa}^{2}=\frac{2}{k}(1+c)\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2},

where we used that
u_{i}^{2}=1/k^{2}
for every
i
. Therefore

\frac{4\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}(1-c)^{2}}{k^{2}}\leq\|\mu_{1,k}-\mu_{2,k}\|_{\kappa}^{4}\leq\frac{4\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}(1+c)^{2}}{k^{2}}.

(92)

Bounding
(\bm{u}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}
.
Since

u_{i_{1}}u_{i_{2}}=(-1)^{i_{1}+i_{2}}/k^{2}
for each
i_{1},i_{2}\in[2k]
, we write

(\bm{u}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}=\frac{1}{k^{4}}\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[2k]}(-1)^{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+i_{4}}\Big[\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\Big]_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}.

Consider the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{=}

\displaystyle:=\{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in[2k]^{4},\>\>i_{1}-i_{2}=i_{3}-i_{4}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{+}

\displaystyle:=\{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in[2k]^{4},\>(-1)^{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+i_{4}}=1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{-}

\displaystyle:=\{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in[2k]^{4},\>(-1)^{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+i_{4}}=-1\},

and observe that
\mathcal{I}_{=}\subset\mathcal{I}_{+}
,
\mathcal{I}_{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{-}=[2k]^{4}
and that the definition (
90
) implies

\displaystyle(-1)^{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+i_{4}}\Big[\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\Big]_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}\kappa_{w}(0,0),&\forall(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\mathcal{I}_{=}\\
-|\kappa_{w}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}},\theta_{i_{4}}-\theta_{i_{3}})|&\forall(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\mathcal{I}_{-}\\
-|\kappa_{w}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}},\theta_{i_{4}}-\theta_{i_{3}})|&\forall(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\mathcal{I}_{+}\backslash\mathcal{I}_{=}.\end{cases}

(93)

Further observe that
since
i_{1}-i_{2}-i_{3}+i_{4}\equiv i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+i_{4}[2]
, if
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\mathcal{I}_{-}\cup(\mathcal{I}_{+}\backslash\mathcal{I}_{=})
, then either
i_{1}-i_{2}-i_{3}+i_{4}\equiv 1[2]
or
i_{1}-i_{2}-i_{3}+i_{4}\neq 0
.
In both cases, we have
|i_{1}-i_{2}-i_{3}+i_{4}|\geq 1
, hence

\|\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}}-\theta_{i_{3}}+\theta_{i_{4}}\|=\|(i_{1}-i_{2}-i_{3}+i_{4})\theta^{*}\|\geq 1,
and

|\kappa_{w}(\theta_{i_{1}}-\theta_{i_{2}},\theta_{i_{3}}-\theta_{i_{4}})|\leq\sup_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}:\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\geq 1}|\kappa_{w}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})|\leq\frac{c_{w}}{2k}\kappa_{w}(0,0),

where we used definition (
85
).
Observe moreover that
since
\mathcal{I}_{-}=\{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4});(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})\in[2k]^{3},i_{4}\in[2k],i_{4}\equiv 1-(i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3})[2]\}
we have
\sharp\mathcal{I}_{-}=(2k)^{3}\times k=8k^{4}
. Similarly,
\sharp(\mathcal{I}_{+}\backslash\mathcal{I}_{=})=\sharp\mathcal{I}_{+}-\sharp\mathcal{I}_{=}=8k^{4}-\sharp\mathcal{I}_{=}
. Finally, as we will show below
\sharp\mathcal{I}_{=}\geq 16k^{3}/3
(the proof is postponed at the end of the section) we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in[2k]}(-1)^{i_{1}+i_{2}+i_{3}+i_{4}}\Big[\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\Big]_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\Big(\sharp\mathcal{I}_{=}-\sharp(\mathcal{I}_{+}\backslash\mathcal{I}_{=})\cdot\frac{c_{w}}{2k}-\sharp\mathcal{I}_{-}\cdot\frac{c_{w}}{2k}\Big)\cdot\kappa_{w}(0,0)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sharp\mathcal{I}_{=}\Big(1+\frac{c_{w}}{2k}\Big)-8c_{w}k^{3}\Big)\cdot\kappa_{w}(0,0)

\displaystyle\geq(16/3-8c_{w})k^{3}\cdot\kappa_{w}(0,0).

Thus,

(\bm{u}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}\bm{K}_{w}(\theta)\bm{u}^{\otimes 2}\geq\frac{8}{k}\Big(\frac{2}{3}-c_{w}\Big)\cdot\kappa_{w}(0,0)

and combining with (
91
) and (
92
) yields

\mathbb{V}\|\mathcal{A}\nu_{k}\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{m}(C_{w}k-1)
with

C_{w}k=\frac{8}{k}(2/3-c_{w})\kappa_{w}(0,0)\frac{k^{2}}{4\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}(1+c)^{2}},

i.e.,
C_{w}=2(2/3-c_{w})\frac{\kappa_{w}(0,0)}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{4}(1+c)^{2}}
as defined in (
85
).

To conclude the proof of (
84
) we now establish the claimed lower bound on
\sharp\mathcal{I}_{=}
. Since
\mathcal{I}_{=}
is the disjoint union of
\mathcal{I}^{\ell}_{=}:=\{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in[2k]^{4},i_{1}-i_{2}=i_{3}-i_{4}=\ell\}
,
\ell\in\{-(2k-1),\ldots,0,\ldots,2k-1\}
, and
\sharp\mathcal{I}^{\ell}_{=}=\sharp\mathcal{I}^{-\ell}_{=}=(2k-\ell)^{2}
for
0\leq\ell\leq 2k-1
, hence

\displaystyle\sharp\mathcal{I}_{=}

\displaystyle=\sharp\mathcal{I}^{0}_{=}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{2k-1}\sharp\mathcal{I}^{\ell}_{=}=(2k)^{2}+\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{2k-1}2(2k-\ell)^{2}=\Big(2\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{2k-1}(2k-\ell)^{2}\Big)-(2k)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(2\sum\limits_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{2k}(\ell^{\prime})^{2}\Big)-4k^{2}=\frac{1}{3}2k(2k+1)(4k+1)-\frac{12}{3}k^{2}\geq\frac{16}{3}k^{3}.

(94)

where we used the well known fact that
\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{n}(\ell^{\prime})^{2}=n(n+1)(2n+1)/6
for every integer
n
.

We proceed to the second claim. For
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
such that
\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\geq 1
,
\iota=\pi_{\theta}
and
\iota^{\prime}=\pi_{\theta^{\prime}}
are (non-normalized)
1
-separated dipoles with respect to
\varrho
. The mutual coherence assumption and Lemma
30
yield

\frac{\kappa_{w_{0}}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}=\langle\pi_{\theta},\pi_{\theta^{\prime}}\rangle_{\kappa}\leq\frac{c}{2k}\|\pi_{\theta}\|_{\kappa}\|\pi_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{\kappa}=\frac{c}{2k}\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}=\frac{c}{2k}\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{0}\rangle_{\kappa}=\frac{c}{2k}\frac{\kappa_{w_{0}}(0,0)}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}.

This shows that
c_{w_{0}}
defined in (
85
) satisfies
c_{w_{0}}\leq c
.

A.3
Proof of Proposition
15

For any mixture model, kernel, and feature family, the set of normalized dipoles
\mathfrak{D}
is included in the normalized secant set hence

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{F}}=\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}|\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\nu\rangle|\geq\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sup_{\nu\in\mathfrak{D}}|\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\nu\rangle|.

For
x\in\Theta
s.t.
0<\|x\|\leq 1
, define
\tilde{\iota}_{x}:=\pi_{x}-\pi_{0}
. Since
\Theta
contains a neighborhood of zero,
\pi_{0}/\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa},\tilde{\iota}_{x}/\|\tilde{\iota}_{x}\|_{\kappa}\in\mathfrak{D}
as soon as
\|x\|
is small enough hence there is
0<\delta\leq 1
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{F}}\geq\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max\left(\frac{|\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle|}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}},\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\frac{|\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\pi_{x}-\pi_{0}\rangle|}{\|\pi_{x}-\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}}\right).

Since
\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\pi_{x}\rangle=e^{\imath\langle\omega,x\rangle}\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle
and
\|\pi_{x}-\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}=2\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))
we have

\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\frac{|\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\pi_{x}-\pi_{0}\rangle|}{\|\pi_{x}-\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}}=|\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle|\cdot\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\frac{|e^{\imath\langle\omega,x\rangle}-1|}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}\sqrt{2(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}}.

Now, since
\bar{\kappa}
achieved its maximum at zero where it is
C^{2}
, we have for every
x\neq 0

\lim_{t\to 0}\sqrt{\frac{1-\bar{\kappa}(tx)}{t^{2}\|x\|^{2}}}=\sqrt{\frac{-x^{\mathrm{T}}\nabla^{2}\bar{\kappa}(0)x}{2\|x\|^{2}}}.

By assumption,
\nabla^{2}\bar{\kappa}(0)\neq 0
and we get

C:=\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\lim_{t\to 0}\sqrt{\frac{1-\bar{\kappa}(tx)}{t^{2}\|x\|^{2}}}=\sqrt{\frac{\|\nabla^{2}\bar{\kappa}(0)\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{2}}>0.

We obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\frac{|e^{\imath\langle\omega,x\rangle}-1|}{\sqrt{1-\bar{\kappa}(x)}}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{|e^{\imath t\langle\omega,x\rangle}-1|}{\sqrt{1-\bar{\kappa}(tx)}}=\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{|\imath t\langle\omega,x\rangle|}{\sqrt{1-\bar{\kappa}(tx)}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}\frac{|\langle\omega,x/\|x\|\rangle|}{\lim_{t\to 0}\sqrt{\frac{1-\bar{\kappa}(tx)}{t^{2}\|x\|^{2}}}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\sup_{0<\|x\|\leq\delta}|\langle\omega,x/\|x\|\rangle|=\frac{1}{C}\|\omega\|_{\star}.

Therefore

\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{F}}\geq\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\langle\phi^{w}_{\omega},\pi_{0}\rangle|}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}}\max\left(1,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}C}\|\omega\|_{\star}\right).

To conclude we use that
(\sqrt{2}C)^{-1}=1/\sqrt{\|\nabla^{2}\bar{\kappa}(0)\|_{\mathrm{op}}}
.

A.4
Proof of
Proposition

16

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
. By
Proposition

6
, there exist
2k
normalized dipoles
\iota_{1},\dots,\iota_{2k}\in\mathfrak{D}
, with
(\iota_{i},\iota_{j})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}
when
i\neq j
, and coefficients
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2k}\geq 0
such that

(1+c)^{-1}\leq\sum_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq(1-c)^{-1}
that satisfy

\nu=\sum_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\iota_{i}
.
We notice that

\sum\limits_{i\neq j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}=(\sum_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i})^{2}-\sum_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq(\sqrt{2k}\|\alpha\|_{2})^{2}-\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2k-1}{1-c}.

Since
\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta}
is well defined for probability distributions in the family
\mathcal{T}
, the action of
\mathcal{A}
is well defined on
k
-mixtures, hence on elements of the normalized secant set. We have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}\nu\|_{2}^{2}-\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i,j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle-\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}(\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-1)+\sum\limits_{i\neq j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle,

we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}\nu\|_{2}^{2}-1

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}-1+\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}(\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-1)+\sum\limits_{i\neq j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle.

For
i\neq j
, since
\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{j},\mathcal{A}\iota_{i}\rangle
is the complex conjugate of
\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle
, we have
\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle+\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{j},\mathcal{A}\iota_{i}\rangle=\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle+\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{j},\mathcal{A}\iota_{i}\rangle
hence

\sum\limits_{i\neq j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle=\sum\limits_{i\neq j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle
.
As a result

\displaystyle\big|\|\mathcal{A}\nu\|_{2}^{2}-1\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|1-\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}\big|+\Big|\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}(\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-1)\Big|+\Big|\sum\limits_{i\neq j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota_{i},\mathcal{A}\iota_{j}\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|1-\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}\big|+\sum\limits_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}\sup\limits_{\iota\in\mathfrak{D}}\big|\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}-1\big|+\sum\limits_{i\neq j\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\sup\limits_{(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}}\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle\big|.

Now, since
(1+c)^{-1}\leq\sum_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}\leq(1-c)^{-1}

we have
\big|1-\sum_{i\in[2k]}\alpha_{i}^{2}\big|\leq c/(1-c)
, hence

\displaystyle\big|\|\mathcal{A}\nu\|_{2}^{2}-1\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-c}\Bigg(c+\sup\limits_{\iota\in\mathfrak{D}}\big|\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}-1\big|+(2k-1)\sup\limits_{(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}}\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle\big|\Bigg).

Since this holds for every
\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
, this establishes (
29
) using the definitions of
\delta(\cdot|\mathcal{A})
(see (
9
)) and of
\mu(\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A})
(see (
14
)).
∎

A.5
Proof of
Proposition

18

To prove
Proposition

18
we rely on a generic formula expressing
\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}
for
\iota\in\mathfrak{D}
that depends on a scalar
\alpha\in[0,1]
, which reflects how
balanced
the normalized dipole
\iota
is, and on a vector
x
, which reflects the
relative position
between the supports of the two monopoles that form
\iota
. We also exploit an expression of
\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle
for
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}
that depends on two scalars
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
reflecting how balanced
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
are respectively, and on two relative-position vectors
x,x^{\prime}
.
The proof of the following proposition is deferred to
Section

A.5.1
.

Proposition 34
.

Consider
\mathcal{T}=(\Theta,\rho,\mathcal{I})
a location-based family with base distribution
\pi_{0}
where
\rho(\cdot,\cdot)=\|\cdot-\cdot\|
for some norm
\|\cdot\|
, and
\kappa
a normalized shift-invariant kernel that is locally characteristic with respect to
\mathcal{T}
. Consider

\mathcal{A}
a WFF sketching operator (
Definition

10
) with frequencies
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{m}
,
\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\psi(\omega)
as defined in (
43
) and (
36
), and
\bar{\kappa}
as defined in (
11
).

•

For any normalized dipole
\iota\in\mathfrak{D}
, there exists
\alpha\in[0,1]
and a vector
x\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}

such that

\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\frac{(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\cos\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle)}{(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}.

(95)

The case of a normalized monopole
\iota\in\mathfrak{M}
corresponds to
\alpha=0
(and arbitrary
x
), while the case of a balanced normalized dipole corresponds to
\alpha=1
.
Vice-versa, for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
x\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
there is
\iota\in\mathfrak{D}
such that this equality holds.

•

For
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}
, there exist
s,s^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime}\in\Theta
that satisfy

\displaystyle\forall i,j\in\{1,2\},

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>\varrho(\theta_{i},\theta_{j}^{\prime})\geq 1,

\displaystyle 0<\varrho(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\leq 1,

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>0<\varrho(\theta_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime})\leq 1,

(96)

such that

\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle=ss^{\prime}\frac{a-\alpha^{\prime}b-\alpha c+\alpha\alpha^{\prime}d}{\sqrt{(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}\sqrt{(1-\alpha^{\prime})^{2}+2\alpha^{\prime}(1-\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime}))}},

(97)

where
x:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
and
x^{\prime}:=\theta_{1}^{\prime}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
, and
a,b,c
and
d
are defined as follows

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{1}},\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\prime}}\rangle,

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{1}},\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\prime}}\rangle,

(98)

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{2}},\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\prime}}\rangle,

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{2}},\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\prime}}\rangle.

The case where
\iota
is a monopole (resp. a balanced dipole) corresponds to
\alpha=0
(resp.
\alpha=1
) and similarly for
\iota^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}
.

Proof of (
33
).

Since normalized monopoles and balanced normalized dipoles are special cases of normalized dipoles, we have
\mathfrak{M}\subset\mathfrak{D}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}\subset\mathfrak{D}
hence by the definition (
9
) of
\delta(\mathfrak{D}|\mathcal{A})
as a supremum we trivially have

\delta(\mathfrak{D}|\mathcal{A})\geq\max\big(\delta(\mathfrak{M}|\mathcal{A}),\delta(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}|\mathcal{A})\big).

To establish (
33
) we show the converse inequality.
First observe that for any normalized monopole
\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\in\mathfrak{M}
we have

\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})
. Since
\varrho
is a norm, this is a direct consequence of Proposition
34
and shows that
\delta(\mathfrak{M}|\mathcal{A})=|1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2}|
independently of the choice of the monopole
\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}
.

Now, consider an arbitrary normalized dipole
\iota\in\mathfrak{D}
.
If
\iota
is either a normalized monopole or a balanced dipole, we trivially have
|1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}|\leq\max(\delta(\mathfrak{M}|\mathcal{A}),\delta(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}|\mathcal{A}))
. Otherwise,
by Proposition
34
again, there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that

\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\frac{(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\cos\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle)}{(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}.

Define a
balanced
normalized dipole as

\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}:=\frac{\pi_{0}-\pi_{x}}{\|\pi_{0}-\pi_{x}\|_{\kappa}}\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}
.
Proposition
34
again yields

\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\frac{1-\cos\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle}{1-\bar{\kappa}(x)}

so that, with simple algebraic manipulations, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\alpha)^{2}\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})+2\alpha\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})(1-\cos\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle)}{(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\alpha)^{2}\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}}{(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}.

We deduce that

\min\big(\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2},\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}\big)\leq\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}\leq\max\big(\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2},\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}\big).

Moreover, since
\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}
, we have

\max(1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2},\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}-1)=|1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}|\leq\delta(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}|\mathcal{A})

hence

\displaystyle 1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\min\big(\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2},\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\max\big(1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2},1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}\big)\leq\max\big(\delta(\mathfrak{M}|\mathcal{A}),\delta(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}|\mathcal{A})\big),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}-1

\displaystyle\leq\max\big(\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\mathfrak{M}}\|_{2}^{2}-1,\|\mathcal{A}\iota_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}\|_{2}^{2}-1\big)\leq\max\big(\delta(\mathfrak{M}|\mathcal{A}),\delta(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}|\mathcal{A})\big),

This shows that
|1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}|\leq\max\big(\delta(\mathfrak{M}|\mathcal{A}),\delta(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}|\mathcal{A})\big)
and establishes (
33
) as claimed. ∎

Proof of (
34
).

Recall that the families defined in (
32
) satisfy

\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2},\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}\subset\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}
. Hence, by the definition (
14
) of
\mu(\cdot|\mathcal{A})
as a supremum we trivially have

\mu(\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A})\geq\max\big(\mu(\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A}),\mu(\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}|\mathcal{A}),\mu(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A})\big).

This yields the lower bound in (
34
). To establish the upper bound we will use a result which proof is postponed to Section
A.5.2
.

Proposition 35
.

Let
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}
and
e,f\in[0,1)
and consider the function
g
defined on
[0,1]\times[0,1]
by

h(u,v)=\frac{a-bu-cv+duv}{\sqrt{1+u^{2}-2eu}\sqrt{1+v^{2}-2fv}}.

(99)

We have

\sup\limits_{(u,v)\in[0,1]^{2}}\>\>\big|h(u,v)\big|\leq 3\max\Big(|a|,|b|,|c|,|d|,\frac{|b-a|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|d-c|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|d-b|}{\sqrt{1-f}},\frac{|c-a|}{\sqrt{1-f}},\frac{|a-b-c+d|}{\sqrt{1-e}\sqrt{1-f}}\Big).

Consider an arbitrary
1
-separated pair of normalized dipoles,
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}
, and denote
s,s^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime}\in\Theta
,
x
,
a,b,c,d
the parameters satisfying (
•
‣
34
)-(
97
)-(
98
) as given by Proposition
34
. As
x,x^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
we have
0<\varrho(x,0)\leq 1
and
0<\varrho(x^{\prime},0)\leq 1
, and since
\kappa
is
locally characteristic

13
13

13

See Definition
3
.
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
and as
\kappa\geq 0
implies
\bar{\kappa}\geq 0
(cf (
7
) and (
11
)) , this yields
e:=\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime}),f:=\bar{\kappa}(x)\in[0,1)

so that
the expression (
97
) reads as
h(\alpha^{\prime},\alpha)
with
h
as in (
99
).
Since
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1]
and
e,f\in[0,1)
, by Proposition
35
applied to the absolute value of the expression (
97
) we get

\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle\big|\leq 3\max\Big(|a|,|b|,|c|,|d|,\frac{|b-a|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|c-d|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|b-d|}{\sqrt{1-f}},\frac{|a-c|}{\sqrt{1-f}},\frac{|b-a-c+d|}{\sqrt{1-e}\sqrt{1-f}}\Big).

Now, observe that (
98
) in Proposition
34
implies that every
t\in\{a,b,c,d\}
can be written as
t=\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\nu,\mathcal{A}\nu^{\prime}\rangle
where

(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}
, hence

\max(|a|,|b|,|c|,|d|)\leq\sup\limits_{(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}}\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\xi,\mathcal{A}\xi^{\prime}\rangle\big|=\mu(\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A}).

Similarly, observe that (
98
) implies also that

|b-a|/\sqrt{1-e}=|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\nu,\mathcal{A}\nu^{\prime}\rangle|
where

\nu:=\frac{\pi_{\theta_{1}}}{\|\pi_{\theta_{1}}\|_{\kappa}}\in\mathfrak{M};\>\>\>\>\nu^{\prime}:=\frac{\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\prime}}-\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\prime}}}{\|\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\prime}}-\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\prime}}\|_{\kappa}}\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}},

and by (
•
‣
34
) we have
(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}
hence

\frac{|b-a|}{\sqrt{1-e}}\leq\sup\limits_{(xi,\xi^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}}\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\xi,\mathcal{A}\xi^{\prime}\rangle\big|=:\mu(\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}|\mathcal{A}).

By symmetry, the same argument is valid for
|c-d|/\sqrt{1-e},|b-d|/\sqrt{1-f}
and
|a-c|/\sqrt{1-f}
. Therefore

\max\Big(\frac{|b-a|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|c-d|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|b-d|}{\sqrt{1-f}},\frac{|a-c|}{\sqrt{1-f}}\Big)\leq\mu(\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}|\mathcal{A}).

Finally, with
\nu:=(\pi_{\theta_{1}}-\pi_{\theta_{2}})/\|\pi_{\theta_{1}}-\pi_{\theta_{2}}\|_{\kappa}
and
\nu^{\prime}:=(\pi_{\theta^{\prime}_{1}}-\pi_{\theta^{\prime}_{2}})/\|\pi_{\theta^{\prime}_{1}}-\pi_{\theta^{\prime}_{2}}\|_{\kappa}
, we have
(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}
(by (
•
‣
34
)) and as a result

\frac{|b-a-c+d|}{\sqrt{1-e}\sqrt{1-f}}=\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\nu,\mathcal{A}\nu^{\prime}\rangle\big|\leq\sup\limits_{(\xi,\xi^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}}\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\xi,\mathcal{A}\xi^{\prime}\rangle\big|=:\mu(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A}).

Combining all of the above yields

\big|\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle\big|\leq 3\max\big(\mu(\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A}),\mu(\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}|\mathcal{A}),\mu(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}|\mathcal{A})\big).

As this holds for every
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}
this establishes (
34
).∎

A.5.1
Proof of Proposition
34

Consider a normalized dipole
\iota\in\mathfrak{D}
. Since
\rho
is a norm we can apply Lemma C.1 in
(
Gribonval et al. 2021a
)
hence there exists a dipole
\tilde{\iota}
such that
\iota=\frac{\tilde{\iota}}{\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}}
, with

\tilde{\iota}=\frac{s}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}}(\pi_{\theta_{1}}-\alpha\pi_{\theta_{2}})
,
where
s\in\{-1,1\}
,
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
x:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\in\Theta-\Theta
satisfies
0<\|x\|\leq 1
. Since
\kappa
is locally characteristic we have
\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}>0
hence the ratio
\tilde{\iota}/\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}
indeed makes sense. The case of a normalized monopole
\iota\in\mathfrak{M}
corresponds to
\alpha=0
and an arbitrary
x
, while the case of a balanced dipole corresponds to
\alpha=1
.
Moreover, since
\kappa
is translation invariant and
\mathcal{T}
is a location-based family by (
16
) we have

\|\pi_{\theta_{1}}\|_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{\theta_{2}}\|_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}
,
and

\langle\pi_{\theta_{1}},\pi_{\theta_{2}}\rangle_{\kappa}=\bar{\kappa}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\cdot\|\pi_{\theta_{1}}\|_{\kappa}\|\pi_{\theta_{2}}\|_{\kappa}=\bar{\kappa}(x)\cdot\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}
where we recall that the
\mathcal{T}
-normalized kernel
\bar{\kappa}
is defined in (
11
).
Therefore

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x)),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}\tilde{\iota}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\Big((1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\cos\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle)\Big).

Since
\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=\|\mathcal{A}\tilde{\iota}\|_{2}^{2}/\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}^{2}
, taking the quotient yields (
95
) as claimed.
Vice-versa for
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
x\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
, there are
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Theta^{2}
such that
0<\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|\leq 1
and setting
\iota=(\pi_{\theta_{1}}-\alpha\pi_{\theta_{2}})/\|\pi_{\theta_{1}}-\alpha\pi_{\theta_{2}}\|_{\kappa}
yields a normalized dipole satisfying the desired expression.

Similarly, for any
1
-separated pair of normalized dipoles
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}

we write
\iota=\frac{\tilde{\iota}}{\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}},\>\>\iota^{\prime}=\frac{\tilde{\iota}^{\prime}}{\|\tilde{\iota}^{\prime}\|_{\kappa}}
,
with

\tilde{\iota}=\frac{s}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}}(\pi_{\theta_{1}}-\alpha\pi_{\theta_{2}}),\>\>\tilde{\iota}^{\prime}=\frac{s^{\prime}}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}}(\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\prime}}-\alpha^{\prime}\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\prime}}),

where
s,s^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1]
and
x:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\in\Theta-\Theta
and
x^{\prime}:=\theta_{1}^{\prime}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\in\Theta-\Theta
satisfy
0<\|x\|\leq 1
and
0<\|x^{\prime}\|\leq 1
. The
1
-separation assumption means that for every

i,j\in\{1,2\}
we have
\|\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}^{\prime}\|\geq 1
. Since

\|\pi_{\theta_{1}}\|_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{\theta_{2}}\|_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\prime}}\|_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}

and

\langle\pi_{\theta_{1}},\pi_{\theta_{2}}\rangle_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}\bar{\kappa}(x)
,
and

\langle\pi_{\theta_{1}^{\prime}},\pi_{\theta_{2}^{\prime}}\rangle_{\kappa}=\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime})

we obtain

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)^{2}+2\alpha(1-\bar{\kappa}(x)),

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\iota}^{\prime}\|_{\kappa}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha^{\prime})^{2}+2\alpha^{\prime}(1-\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\big(\langle\mathcal{A}\tilde{\iota},\mathcal{A}\tilde{\iota}^{\prime}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{ss^{\prime}}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}\mathfrak{Re}\Big(\big\langle\mathcal{A}(\pi_{\theta_{1}}-\alpha\pi_{\theta_{2}}),\mathcal{A}(\pi_{\theta_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}}-\alpha\pi_{\theta_{2}^{{}^{\prime}}})\big\rangle\Big)=ss^{\prime}\left(a-\alpha^{\prime}b-\alpha c+\alpha\alpha^{\prime}d\right),

with
a,b,c,d
as in (
98
).
Since
\mathfrak{Re}\big(\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle\big)=\mathfrak{Re}\big(\langle\mathcal{A}\tilde{\iota},\mathcal{A}\tilde{\iota}^{\prime}\rangle\big)/(\|\tilde{\iota}\|_{\kappa}\|\tilde{\iota}^{\prime}\|_{\kappa})
we obtain (
97
).
The special cases of monopoles and balanced dipoles,
are proved similarly as above and left to the reader.
∎

A.5.2
Proof of Proposition
35

We will use a technical Lemma which proof is postponed to the end of the section.

Lemma 36
.

Consider
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma\in[0,1)
and the function
\phi_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
defined on
[0,1]
by

\phi_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)=\frac{\alpha-\beta t}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}-2\gamma t}}.

We have

\forall t\in[0,1],\>\>|\phi_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)|\leq\sqrt{3}\max\Big(|\alpha|,|\beta|,\frac{|\beta-\alpha|}{\sqrt{2(1-\gamma)}}\Big).

Consider
(u,v)\in[0,1]^{2}
,
f\in[0,1)
, and define
g:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+v^{2}-2fv}}
. We have

h(u,v)=\frac{ga-gbu-gcv+gduv}{\sqrt{1+u^{2}-2eu}}=\frac{(ga-gcv)-(gb-gdv)u}{\sqrt{1+u^{2}-2eu}}=\phi_{ga-gcv,gb-gdv,e}(u)

hence, by Lemma
36

\displaystyle|h(u,v)|

\displaystyle=|\phi_{ga-gcv,gb-gdv,e}(u)|\leq\sqrt{3}\max\Big(|ga-gcv|,|gb-gdv|,\frac{|gb-gdv-ga+gcv|}{\sqrt{1-e}}\Big).

Now, we can use Lemma
36
again to get

\displaystyle|ga-gcv|

\displaystyle=\frac{|a-cv|}{\sqrt{1+v^{2}-2fv}}=|\phi_{a,c,f}(v)|\leq\sqrt{3}\max\Big(|a|,|c|,\frac{|c-a|}{\sqrt{1-f}}\Big),

\displaystyle|gb-gdv|

\displaystyle=\frac{|b-dv|}{\sqrt{1+v^{2}-2fv}}=|\phi_{b,d,f}(v)|\leq\sqrt{3}\max\Big(|b|,|d|,\frac{|d-b|}{\sqrt{1-f}}\Big),

\displaystyle|gb-gdv-ga+gcv|

\displaystyle=|gb-ga+(gc-gd)v|=\frac{|b-a-(d-c)v|}{\sqrt{1+v^{2}-2fv}}=|\phi_{b-a,d-c,f}(v)|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{3}\max\Big(|b-a|,|d-c|,\frac{|(d-c)-(b-a)|}{\sqrt{1-f}}\Big).

Combining the above inequalities
we obtain

|h(u,v)|\leq 3\max\Big(|a|,|b|,|c|,|d|,\frac{|b-a|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|d-c|}{\sqrt{1-e}},\frac{|d-b|}{\sqrt{1-f}},\frac{|c-a|}{\sqrt{1-f}},\frac{|a-b-c+d|}{\sqrt{1-e}\sqrt{1-f}}\Big).\qed

Proof of Lemma
36
.

Equivalently, we bound
c:=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}g(t)
where
g(t):=|\phi_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t)|^{2}=P(t)/Q(t)
,
P(t):=(\beta t-\alpha)^{2}
, and
Q(t):=1+t^{2}-2\gamma t
. The bound
c\leq 3\max(\alpha^{2},\beta^{2},(\beta-\alpha)^{2}/(1-\gamma))
is trivial if
\alpha=\beta=0
, so we now assume
(\alpha,\beta)\neq 0
.
We have
g(0)=\alpha^{2}
and
g(1)=(\beta-\alpha)^{2}/(2(1-\gamma))
so the bound is also trivial if the maximum is achieved at a boundary point, so to conclude we now assume that
c
is achieved at an interior point
t\in(0,1)
, which must satisfy
g^{\prime}(t)=0
. Since
g^{\prime}=(P^{\prime}Q-PQ^{\prime})/Q^{2}
the fact that
g^{\prime}(t)=0
reads as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=P^{\prime}(t)Q(t)-P(t)Q^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=2\beta(\beta t-\alpha)(1+t^{2}-2\gamma t)-(\beta t-\alpha)^{2}2(t-\gamma)=2(\beta t-\alpha)\big(\beta(1+t^{2}-2\gamma t)-(\beta t-\alpha)(t-\gamma)\big)

\displaystyle=2(\beta t-\alpha)\big(\beta+\bcancel{\beta t^{2}}-2\beta\gamma t-\bcancel{\beta t^{2}}+\alpha t+\beta\gamma t-\alpha\gamma\big)=2(\beta t-\alpha)\big((\alpha-\beta\gamma)t-(\alpha\gamma-\beta)\big).

Since we assume
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0)
, we have
P(t)/Q(t)=g(t)\geq\max(g(0),g(1))=\max(\alpha^{2},(\beta-\alpha)^{2}/(2(1-\gamma)))>0
, hence
P(t)\neq 0
, i.e.
\beta t-\alpha\neq 0
, thus the location of the maximum satisfies

(\alpha-\beta\gamma)t=\alpha\gamma-\beta.

This implies that
\alpha\neq\beta\gamma
(otherwise we would have both
\alpha=\beta\gamma
and
\alpha\gamma=\beta
, hence
\beta=\alpha\gamma=(\beta\gamma)\gamma=\beta\gamma^{2}
, and similarly
\alpha=\alpha\gamma^{2}
; since
0\leq\gamma<1
this would contradict the fact that
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0)
).
Moreover, since
P^{\prime}(t)Q(t)-P(t)Q^{\prime}(t)=0
we have
g(t)=P(t)/Q(t)=P^{\prime}(t)/Q^{\prime}(t)=2\beta(\beta t-\alpha)/(2(t-\gamma))
. Since
g(t)>0
this shows that
\beta\neq 0
, and we conclude that

\displaystyle g(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta(\beta t-\alpha)}{t-\gamma}=\beta\frac{(\alpha-\beta\gamma)(\beta t-\alpha)}{(\alpha-\beta\gamma)(t-\gamma)}=\beta\frac{(\alpha\gamma-\beta)\beta-(\alpha-\beta\gamma)\alpha}{(\alpha\gamma-\beta)-(\alpha-\beta\gamma)\gamma}

\displaystyle=\beta\frac{2\alpha\beta\gamma-\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}}{\beta(\gamma^{2}-1)}=\frac{\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}-2\alpha\beta\gamma}{1-\gamma^{2}}=\frac{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}+2\alpha\beta(1-\gamma)}{(1+\gamma)(1-\gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}{1-\gamma}+2|\alpha\beta|\leq\frac{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}{1-\gamma}+2\max\left(\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}\right)\leq 3\max\left(\alpha^{2},\beta^{2},\frac{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}{1-\gamma}\right).

∎

A.6
Proof of Proposition
19

Equalities (
42
), (
43
), (
44
), (
45
) and (
46
) are straightforward applications of the following lemma.

Lemma 37
.

Under the assumptions and notations of Proposition
19
, there exist sets
\Theta_{\mathrm{md}}\subset\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}\times\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
and
\Theta_{\mathrm{dd}}\subset\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}\times\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}\times\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
such that,

1.

For every
\iota\in\mathfrak{M}
, we have
1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=1-\Psi_{\mathrm{m}}(\bm{\Omega})
, for every
\bm{\Omega}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\Omega}}
.

2.

For every
\iota\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}
, there is
x\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
such that
1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=1-\Psi_{\mathrm{d}}(x|\bm{\Omega})
for every
\bm{\Omega}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\Omega}}
; and vice-versa for every
x\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
, there is
\iota\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}
such that
1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=1-\Psi_{\mathrm{d}}(x|\bm{\Omega})
for every
\bm{\Omega}
.

3.

For
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}
, there are
s\in\{-1,1\}
,
y\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
s.t.
\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle=s\Psi_{\mathrm{mm}}(y|\bm{\Omega})
for every
\bm{\Omega}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\Omega}}
, and vice-versa.

4.

For
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}
there are
s\in\{-1,1\}
,
(x,y)\in\Theta_{\mathrm{md}}
s.t.
\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle=s\Psi_{\mathrm{md}}(x,y|\bm{\Omega})
for every
\bm{\Omega}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\Omega}}
, and vice-versa.

5.

For
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}
there are
s\in\{-1,1\}
,
(x,x^{\prime},y)\in\Theta_{\mathrm{dd}}
s.t.
\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle=s\Psi_{\mathrm{dd}}(x,x^{\prime},y|\bm{\Omega})
, for every
\bm{\Omega}
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\Omega}}
, and vice-versa.

Proof of
item 1
:

Proposition
34
yields

\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})

for every
\iota\in\mathfrak{M}
. ∎

Proof of
item 2
:

Proposition
34
yields that for every
\iota\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}
there is
x\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
such that

1-\|\mathcal{A}\iota\|_{2}^{2}=1-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\frac{1-\cos\langle\omega_{j},x\rangle}{1-\bar{\kappa}(x)}=1-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})f_{d}(x|\omega_{j})
, and vice-versa, where we used that for
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have
1-\cos t=2\sin^{2}(t/2)
. ∎

The remaining items use the second part of Proposition
34
which gives a generic formula for
\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle
: when
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{D}_{\neq}^{2}
there are

s,s^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime}\in\Theta
satisfying (
•
‣
34
)-(
97
) with

x:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
,
x^{\prime}:=\theta_{1}^{\prime}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
, and
a,b,c
and
d
are defined by (
98
).
We will also use that given that
\mathcal{A}
is a
(\kappa,w)
-FF sketching operator (cf Definition
10
) and
\pi_{\theta}
is obtained by translation of
\pi_{0}
we have

\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta_{i}},\mathcal{A}\pi_{\theta^{\prime}_{j}}\rangle=\tfrac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|\langle\pi_{0},\phi_{\omega_{j}}\rangle|^{2}\cos\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{i}-\theta^{\prime}_{j}\rangle,

and that
y:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}
satisfies
y\in\Theta-\Theta
and
\|y\|=\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\|\geq 1
by (
•
‣
34
), thus
y\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
.

Proof of
item 3

When
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}
, we have
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}=0

hence (
97
)-(
98
) yield

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=ss^{\prime}a=\frac{ss^{\prime}}{\|\pi_{0}\|_{\kappa}^{2}}\frac{1}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}|\langle\pi_{0},\phi_{\omega_{j}}\rangle|^{2}\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle\big)=\frac{ss^{\prime}}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y\rangle\big).

Vice-versa for such
s,s^{\prime},y
it is easy to exhibit
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\neq}^{2}
satisfying the same expression.∎

Proof of
item 4

When
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}
,
\alpha=0
and
\alpha^{\prime}=1
, which similarly yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=ss^{\prime}\frac{a-b}{\sqrt{2(1-\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime}))}}=\frac{ss^{\prime}}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\frac{\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle\big)}{\sqrt{2(1-\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime}))}}.

Now, using the identity
\cos(u)-\cos(v)=-2\sin((u-v)/2)\sin((u+v)/2)
, we get

\displaystyle\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y+x^{\prime}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=2\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},x^{\prime}\rangle/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},y+x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big).

Thus

\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle=ss^{\prime}\Psi_{\mathrm{md}}((x^{\prime},y)|\bm{\Omega}),

(100)

where
x^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{2}
and
y=\theta_{1}-\theta^{\prime}_{1}
satisfy
x^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
and
y\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
.
We define
\Theta_{\mathrm{md}}
as the set of all couples
(x^{\prime},y)\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}\times\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
that satisfy (
100
) for some
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in(\mathfrak{M}\times\hat{\mathfrak{D}})_{\neq}
and
s,s^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}
.

Proof of
item 5

When
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}
,
\alpha=1
and
\alpha^{\prime}=1
,
hence (
97
)-(
98
) similarly yield

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=ss^{\prime}\frac{a-b-c+d}{\sqrt{2(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}\sqrt{2(1-\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime}))}}

\displaystyle=\frac{ss^{\prime}}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\psi(\omega_{j})\frac{\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle\big)+\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{2}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle\big)}{\sqrt{2(1-\bar{\kappa}(x))}\sqrt{2(1-\bar{\kappa}(x^{\prime}))}}.

Since

\cos(u)-\cos(v)=-2\sin(\frac{u-v}{2})\sin(\frac{u+v}{2})
and

\sin(u)-\sin(v)=2\sin(\frac{u-v}{2})\cos(\frac{u+v}{2})
, and denoting
y:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}
,
x:=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}
, and
x^{\prime}:=\theta_{1}^{\prime}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}
,
we get
x,x^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
,
y\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
and

\displaystyle\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle\big)+\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},\theta_{2}-\theta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y+x^{\prime}\rangle\big)-\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y-x\rangle\big)+\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y-x+x^{\prime}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=2\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},y+x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)-2\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},y-x+x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=2\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)\Big(\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},y+x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)-\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},y-x+x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=4\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},x/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega_{j},x^{\prime}/2\rangle\big)\cos\big(\langle\omega_{j},y+x^{\prime}/2-x/2\rangle\big).

Thus

\mathfrak{Re}\langle\mathcal{A}\iota,\mathcal{A}\iota^{\prime}\rangle=ss^{\prime}\Psi_{\mathrm{dd}}((x,x^{\prime},y)|\bm{\Omega}).

(101)

We define
\Theta_{\mathrm{dd}}
as the set of all triplets
(x,x^{\prime},y)\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}\times\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}\times\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
that satisfy (
101
) for some
(\iota,\iota^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\neq}^{2}
and
s,s^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}
.

A.7
Proof of Theorem
20

Consider
\ell\in\{\mathrm{d},\mathrm{md},\mathrm{mm},\mathrm{dd}\}
and
z,z^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\ell}
, and denote

\Delta_{\ell,\bm{\Omega}}(z,z^{\prime}):=\left|\Psi_{\ell}(z|\bm{\Omega})-\Psi_{\ell}(z^{\prime}|\bm{\Omega})\right|.

The result will follow if we exhibit
z_{1},\ldots,z_{T}
,
2\leq T\leq 6
such that

z_{1}=z
,
z_{T}=z^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq t\leq T-1}\Delta_{\ell}(z_{t},z_{t+1})\leq\Delta_{\ell}(z,z^{\prime})

(102)

and if we can find smooth functions
u\in[0,1]\mapsto f_{\ell,t}(u|\omega)
such that for every
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and every
t

\displaystyle f_{\ell,t}(0|\omega)=f_{\ell}(z_{t}|\omega),

\displaystyle\quad f_{\ell,t}(1|\omega)=f_{\ell}(z_{t+1}|\omega)\,,

(103)

\displaystyle\sup_{0<u<1}|f^{\prime}_{\ell,t}(u|\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq G(\omega)\Delta_{\ell}(z_{t},z_{t+1})

(104)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad G(\omega)

\displaystyle:=C_{\kappa}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\|\omega\|_{a,\star}^{i}.

Indeed, this will imply by the triangle inequality, the mean value theorem, and (
51
) that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\ell,\bm{\Omega}}(z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\ell,\bm{\Omega}}(z_{1},z_{T})\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{\ell,\bm{\Omega}}(z_{t},z_{t+1})=\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\left|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi_{m}(\omega_{j})[f_{\ell,t}(1|\omega_{j})-f_{\ell,t}(0|\omega_{j})]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi_{m}(\omega_{j})\left|[f_{\ell,t}(1|\omega_{j})-f_{\ell,t}(0|\omega_{j})]\right|\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi_{m}(\omega_{j})\sup_{0<u<1}|f^{\prime}_{\ell,t}(u|\omega_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi_{m}(\omega_{j})G(\omega_{j})\Delta_{\ell}(z_{t},z_{t+1})=\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi_{m}(\omega_{j})G(\omega_{j})\right)\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\Delta_{\ell}(z_{t},z_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\psi_{m}(\omega_{j})G(\omega_{j})\right)\cdot 6\Delta_{\ell}(z,z^{\prime})=6\Psi_{0}(\bm{\Omega})\cdot C_{\kappa}\cdot\ \Delta_{\ell}(z,z^{\prime}).

A.7.1
Construction of
z_{t}
,
1\leq t\leq T

First we focus on the construction of
z_{1},\ldots,z_{T}
satifying the inequality (
102
):

•

When
\ell=\mathrm{d}
, we have
z=x
and
z^{\prime}=x^{\prime}
where
x,x^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{d}}
(cf (
43
)). Observe that
r:=\|x\|_{a},r^{\prime}:=\|x^{\prime}\|_{a}
satisfy
r,r^{\prime}>0
, and that
n:=x/r
,
n^{\prime}:=x^{\prime}/r^{\prime}
satisfy
\|n\|_{a}=\|n^{\prime}\|_{a}=1
.
We set
z_{1}=x
,
z_{2}=\tilde{x}
,
z_{3}=x^{\prime}
where
\tilde{x}:=\|x^{\prime}\|_{a}\cdot(x/\|x\|_{a})
satisfies
\|\tilde{x}\|_{a}=\|x^{\prime}\|_{a}
.
Given the definition (
52
) we have
\Delta_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})=|r-r^{\prime}|=|\|x\|_{a}-\|x^{\prime}\|_{a}|\leq\Delta_{\ell}(x,x^{\prime})=\Delta_{\ell}(z,z^{\prime})
and similarly
\Delta_{\ell}(z_{2},z_{3})=\|n-n^{\prime}\|_{a}\leq\Delta_{\ell}(z,z^{\prime})
as claimed.

•

When
\ell=\mathrm{mm}
,
z=y
,
z^{\prime}=y^{\prime}
, where
y,y^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
and we simply set
z_{1}=z
,

.

•

When
\ell=\mathrm{md}
,
z=(x,y)
and

,

. Setting
\tilde{x}
as above we define
z_{1}=z=(x,y)
,
z_{2}=(x,y^{\prime})
,

and

. It is not difficult to check that the inequality (
102
) holds given the definition (
54
) of
\Delta_{\mathrm{md}}
.

•

Finally, when
\ell=\mathrm{dd}
,
z=(x_{1},x_{2},y)
and

where
x_{i}\in\Theta_{d}
and
y,y^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathrm{mm}}
. It is easy to check (
102
) with
z_{1}=(x_{1},x_{2},y)
,
z_{2}=(x_{1},x_{2},y^{\prime})
,
z_{3}=(\tilde{x}_{1},x_{2},y^{\prime})
,
z_{4}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2},y^{\prime})
,
z_{5}=(x_{1}^{\prime},\tilde{x}_{2},y^{\prime})
,
z_{6}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},y^{\prime})
given the definition (
55
) of
\Delta_{\mathrm{dd}}
.

To complete the proof of Theorem

we now exhibit
f_{\ell,t}
satisfying
(
103
)-(
104
) by treating each case

and pair

.

).

A.7.2

satisfying (

The case
\ell=\mathrm{d}
.

For
u\in(0,1)
, we define
\bar{r}(u):=r+u(r^{\prime}-r)
and
\bar{n}(u):=n+u(n^{\prime}-n)
, which satisfies
\|\bar{n}(u)\|_{a}=\|(1-u)n+un^{\prime}\|_{a}\leq(1-u)\|n\|_{a}+u\|n^{\prime}\|_{a}=1
, and we set

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{d},1}(u|\omega)

\displaystyle:=2\frac{\sin^{2}(\langle\omega,\bar{r}(u)n\rangle/2)}{1-\tilde{\kappa}(\bar{r}(u))}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:DefAlphaFn}}}{{=}}2\alpha^{2}(\bar{r}(u))\frac{\sin^{2}(\bar{r}(u)\langle\omega,n\rangle/2)}{(\bar{r}(u))^{2}}\,,

(105)

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{d},2}(u|\omega)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2\sin^{2}(r^{\prime}\langle\omega,\bar{n}(u)\rangle/2)}{1-\tilde{\kappa}(r^{\prime})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:DefAlphaFn}}}{{=}}2\alpha^{2}(r^{\prime})\frac{\sin^{2}(r^{\prime}\langle\omega,\bar{n}(u)\rangle/2)}{(r^{\prime})^{2}}

(106)

Property (

) follows from (
37
) and (

) since

and

so that
\bar{\kappa}(z_{2})=\bar{\kappa}(z_{3})=\tilde{\kappa}(r^{\prime})
.

The case
\ell=\mathrm{mm}
.

For
u\in(0,1)
, we define

and

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{mm},1}(u|\omega)

\displaystyle:=\cos(\langle\omega,\bar{y}(u)\rangle).

Property (
103
) follows by (
38
) since
z_{1}=y=\bar{y}(0)
and

.

The case

For any

, by (

), (

), since

2\sin v\sin w=\cos(v-w)-\cos(v+w)

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{md}}(x,y|\omega)=\sqrt{2}\frac{\sin\big(\langle\omega,x\rangle/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega,y+x/2\rangle\big)}{\sqrt{1-\tilde{\kappa}(\|x\|_{a})}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\alpha(\|x\|_{a})\frac{\sin\big(\langle\omega,x\rangle/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega,y+x/2\rangle\big)}{\|x\|_{a}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\alpha(\|x\|_{a})\frac{\cos(\langle\omega,y\rangle)-\cos(\langle\omega,y\rangle+\langle\omega,x\rangle)}{\|x\|_{a}}.

Since
z_{1}=(x,y)=(x,\bar{y}(0))

, Property (

) holds with

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{2}\alpha(r)\frac{\sin\big(r\langle\omega,n\rangle/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega,\bar{y}(u)+x/2\rangle\big)}{r}\,,

(108)

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{md},2}(u|\omega)

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{2}\alpha(\bar{r}(u))\frac{\sin\big(\bar{r}(u)\langle\omega,n\rangle/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega,y^{\prime}+\bar{r}(u)\cdot n/2\rangle\big)}{\bar{r}(u)}\,,

(109)

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{md},3}(u|\omega)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\alpha(r^{\prime})\frac{\cos(\langle\omega,y^{\prime}\rangle)-\cos(\langle\omega,y^{\prime}\rangle+r^{\prime}\langle\omega,\bar{n}(u)\rangle)}{r^{\prime}}\,.

(110)

)-(

\displaystyle=2\frac{\sin\big(\langle\omega,x_{1}\rangle/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega,x_{2}\rangle/2\big)\cos\big(\langle\omega,y+x_{2}/2-x_{1}/2\rangle\big)}{\sqrt{1-\tilde{\kappa}(\|x_{1}\|_{a})}\sqrt{1-\tilde{\kappa}(\|x_{2}\|_{a})}}

\displaystyle=2\alpha(\|x_{1}\|_{a})\alpha(\|x_{2}\|_{a})\frac{\sin\big(\langle\omega,x_{1}\rangle/2\big)\sin\big(\langle\omega,x_{2}\rangle/2\big)\cos\big(\langle\omega,y+x_{2}/2-x_{1}/2\rangle\big)}{\|x_{1}\|_{a}\|x_{2}\|_{a}}

) holds with
z_{1}=(x_{1},x_{2},y)
